Digital Movement · Web Project Operating Process

Husband Retail — the Research Repository

Version 6.2 · 12 August 2026 · Digital Movement

Mid-fi · internal

Renamed on 12 August 2026. It was called "the search research", which had stopped being true — the file now holds paid search as well, and it is the single place every measured fact about this programme lives. The filename stays 00-sitemap.md: the house gate script and the /seo-sitemap skill both find it by that name, and every other document in the project links to it.

One file, thirteen tabs, in reading order. Executive summary answers what the four markets are, how they differ, and what that means for search and for marketing, in about five minutes. Fit audit is the adversarial second opinion on all of it, and it disagrees. Global markets says which countries are worth opening and in what order. Then one tab per market — Hong Kong first as the home market, then the three measured abroad. Search-term research is the evidence under all of it, Page guide and Page production are how a page gets built, Paid search is what the same evidence says about buying the top of Google instead of earning it, Early verification is superseded history kept for the record, and Sources says where every part came from.

How to read this

  • Searches a month — how many people type that exact phrase into Google in a month. Measured with a research tool, not estimated by us.
  • Competition (0–100) — how much work it takes to reach Google's first page against the firms already sitting there. Under 20 is realistic for a new site. Over 40 is a long job.
  • Checked on the live results page — we opened the real Google results for that phrase, in that country, and read what came back. Where a note says twenty results, personal tailoring off, it means we asked Google for twenty results and told it to forget everything it knows about us, so we saw what any stranger would see. The tool's numbers never decide anything on their own. The results page does.
  • What the searcher wants — who is really typing it. The same words can mean a landlord looking for an adviser, a graduate looking for a job, or a shopper looking for a car park. This is what most of the research time went on.
  • What advertisers pay per click — where a price appears, competing firms are already buying that exact click from Google. It is the clearest proof that money changes hands on a phrase.
  • Fee-earning page and reputation page — a fee-earning page is built to win enquiries. A reputation page explains something; it earns standing, gets quoted (including by AI answers) and lends weight to the fee-earning pages beside it.

Eight of the British pages are held on three decisions only Paul can settle.

The trees do not share vocabulary. Six words that sell in Hong Kong sell something else entirely in America, and in both Australia and Britain "retail agency" means hiring shop staff. Britain is the extreme case: seven different industries have taken phrases that read like this firm's business, and 22,070 searches a month look like the job and are not. Each tab shows its own evidence.

Written 12 August 2026 from the four market tabs in this document. Every figure below is repeated from a tab beside it, where the working is shown. Nothing here is new evidence.

Read the Fit audit tab beside this one before acting on any of it. On 12 August 2026 ten reviewers re-tested every confirmed term against a harder question — not is somebody selling on this results page, but is the searcher looking for what Husband Retail actually sells. Their answer challenges the plan below. Nothing here has been rewritten on the strength of it, because no buyer has yet been asked a single question.

Paul's answer, 12 August 2026 — three decisions closed, and a price on the outcome

Paul confirmed today that tenant representation, property and asset management and valuation and survey are all part of Husband Retail's portfolio, and that the firm wants to hear from people looking for advice on any of the three. He also gave the number this research had been missing: one new project is worth about £100,000.

That settles decisions D7, D8 and D9, which between them were holding 10,540 searches a month across Britain, America and Australia, plus 3,770 a month worldwide in the Hong Kong tree. Britain alone goes from 2,590 searches a month cleared to the full 7,170.

Two things his answer does not settle, and both are checks rather than opinions:

  • Formal valuation is licensed work. In Britain a Red Book valuation may only be signed by an RICS Registered Valuer; in Australia by a Certified Practising Valuer. Advising on value is a different act from signing a valuation. The valuation pages can be written as advisory; they cannot offer the signed product unless the firm has a registered valuer or a named partner firm. This is a legal line, not a positioning one.
  • commercial property management was rejected in the Hong Kong research because the firm supposedly did not sell it. That reason is now void. The term was re-measured on 12 August 2026 and Hong Kong shows 20 searches a month — the research tool holds no Hong Kong results page for it at all. It is dead on demand rather than on fit, and it is now not a search target for Hong Kong. Elsewhere the same phrase is large: America 4,400 a month, Britain 1,600, Australia 1,000, Ireland 1,000.

He also asked for the firm's own work to be published as pages in its own right. That is the /published-work set in the Page guide tab, and it is measured on citations, not positions.

The short version

Four markets were researched to one standard: Hong Kong, the United States, Australia and the United Kingdom. Every term needed three checks before use: monthly searches and a 0-to-100 ranking competition, both from the research tool, and what the searcher actually wants, read from a live Google results page for that country. Miss one and the term was blocked. The reading, all between 10 and 12 August 2026: 108 results pages for Hong Kong; 160 terms measured and 56 pages read for the United States; 145 and 41 for Australia; 130 and 46 for the United Kingdom.

One finding sits above all four markets. The words Husband Retail uses to describe itself are not the words its buyers type. And the same English words mean different businesses in different countries. "Retail consulting" is a fee-earning term in Hong Kong, means McKinsey in America, and in Britain the word "retail" barely belongs to property at all. A single website using one vocabulary would fail in at least three of the four markets.

The four markets side by side

Market Pages (with a search term) Searches a month on those pages Like-for-like, 24 fixed service terms Competition of usable terms What defines it
Hong Kong36 (22)88,020 worldwideMeasured worldwide instead; English service demand is 180Named terms at 20 and 36Home market. The only place where "retail consulting" means property.
United States28 (14, plus 6 secondary phrases)7,40013,09014 of 20 terms at 20 or belowBiggest demand. Consulting and data words belong to other industries.
Australia35 (21)4,2202,7108 to 17Easiest to rank. Least demand for consultancy.
United Kingdom27 (17)6,6403,90024 to 34Most crowded. "Retail" lost to seven other industries.

Britain has the most demand and the hardest terms; Australia has the least demand and the easiest terms. America's obstacle is meaning, not competition. Hong Kong's figure is the least comparable: it is worldwide, and most of it sits on explainer pages.

Market by market

Hong Kong

The home market. Twenty-two of 36 pages carry a term; the rest are about, project and legal pages. The 22 terms carry 88,020 searches a month worldwide. The worldwide basis is deliberate: real Hong Kong demand is in Chinese and could not be measured with the tools available, which was tested and is written up separately. English-language demand on the standard service terms is 180 a month. Only 12,730 of the 88,020 sits on the eight service pages; 75,290 sits on fourteen explainer pages such as "gross leasable area" (35,830 worldwide), which bring authority rather than enquiries. Hong Kong is the only market where "retail consulting" is a genuine fee-earning term (4,000 worldwide, competition 36). The find was "retail property management": 1,150 a month at competition 20.

United States

The biggest market like for like, and easy on paper: fourteen of the twenty confirmed terms score 20 or below for competition, and "landlord representation" scores 1. The risk is meaning. "Retail consulting" in America returns Bain, BCG, McKinsey and Accenture selling store operations to retailers, nothing to do with property; the phrase that works is "commercial real estate consulting", whose results page is nine commercial property consultancies. Data words belong to software firms: "retail analytics" returns Oracle, Mastercard and ThoughtSpot, so a page that works in Hong Kong cannot exist in America. And nine well-known lease terms carry 21,000 searches a month between them, yet not one is worth a page: the results are dictionaries, Wikipedia and law firms, and eight of the nine show zero adverts. The one exception is "cam reconciliation": 590 a month, eight adverts, and a first page of firms selling the work. That is the difference between a word people look up and a problem people pay to solve.

Australia

The thinnest market and the easiest one. The problem is precise: strong demand for retail property services, almost none for retail property consultancy, which is what Husband Retail sells. Thirty-nine measured terms have no Australian demand at all. Generic property words belong to residential buyer's agents: a page built on "property advisory" or similar would rank in front of a private individual buying an investment flat or a first shop. Adding "commercial real estate" fixes it; adding "property" does not. Grammar matters too: "retail leasing agent" returns job adverts, while "retail leasing agents" returns Savills, CBRE and Colliers with no jobs at all. What Australia does offer is the easiest ranking found anywhere: "retail property management" and "tenant representation" both at competition 8.

United Kingdom

All 22 confirmed British terms together carry 7,170 searches a month. Against that, 22,070 British searches a month look exactly like Husband Retail's business and belong to somebody else. That second number is the finding. Britain has given the word "retail" away to seven separate industries: recruitment, TUI's travel-agent login portal, management consultancy, financial services, shop-equipment software, shoppers looking for retail parks, and shop fittings. Exactly two phrases containing "retail" survived every check: "retail property consultants" (90 a month) and "retail planning consultant" (140). "Commercial property" is the prefix that works; nine of the eleven clean terms begin with it or with "commercial real estate". One specialism exists nowhere else, because English planning law makes councils demand a retail impact assessment: "retail planning consultant" (140 a month, competition 7) shows eight advert blocks and nine matching firms out of nine. The lease vocabulary belongs to lawyers: "dilapidations", "rent review", "turnover rent" and "lease advisory" add up to 3,660 searches a month of what looks like the job and is not. Valuation is a matter of law, not positioning: a Red Book valuation may only be signed by an RICS Registered Valuer. And Britain is the hardest: clean terms score 24 to 34 against Australia's 8 to 17.

The pattern in all four

Hong Kong found "retailer representation" at zero searches. America found "retail consulting" meaning McKinsey. Australia returned zero demand for thirty-nine of the firm's own phrases; Britain returned zero for fifteen more, including "shopping centre consultant" and "retail masterplanning". Those words remain correct as a description of the firm. They are not demand. Half the research effort went into throwing away terms with good volume and the wrong audience, because ranking is not the same as being found by a buyer.

What this means for search

Four rules fall out of the evidence. Vocabulary is chosen per market, never carried across; what works in Hong Kong is the wrong page in America. No page is built on a term that has not passed all three checks; volume alone misled in every market. Singular and plural are different search terms and both must be read; one letter separated jobseekers from landlords in Australia. And explainer pages are counted separately from service pages: they carry most of Hong Kong's volume and earn authority and answers in AI search, not enquiries.

What this means for online marketing beyond search

The advert evidence maps where competitors already spend: the highest price recorded in this research is £11.41 a click on Britain's "tenant representation", with A$10.42 on "commercial real estate consulting" in Australia and eight adverts on "cam reconciliation" in America. That is where paid search is worth testing, because someone already pays for exactly that click. Terms with zero adverts, like eight of the nine American lease terms, are not.

The volumes point the other way. The terms that describe buying this work are small in every market. Search will catch existing demand; it will not create demand for a specialist firm, so it should be one channel among several, not the channel.

Publish the work, not the search terms. The strongest asset here is not a phrase anyone types. It is the AI report delivered to Covent Garden's owner, the Mall of Berlin due-diligence brief and Paul's book — work that already exists, is not on the website, and that no competitor can buy at any price. Published, it is what a referred buyer reads before a first meeting, what other sites quote, and what AI assistants repeat when somebody asks who advises shopping centre owners on repositioning. These pages carry no search term and must never be judged on position. They are in the page plan as /published-work, and what may be published is decision D10.

Two faults found on husband-retail.com during this work come before any of it: the HTTPS security certificate is broken, and three of the four service pages still carry Lorem ipsum placeholder text. These undermine every channel at once, and they are the first fix.

What this means commercially

Paul gave the number this research had been missing: one new project is worth about £100,000. That single figure changes the answer more than any search term in this document.

Digital Movement's fee is £3,000 a month — £36,000 a year. The programme breaks even if it produces one new client every two years and ten months. One client a year returns nearly three times the fee. One a quarter returns eleven times it.

That reframes every volume here. Tenant representation in Britain is 140 searches a month — 1,680 a year. That is a small number for a shop and a large one for a firm whose average job is £100,000. The objection "these volumes are too small" only holds if the work is cheap. It is not.

It reframes the adverts too. Competitors pay £11.41 a click on British tenant representation. A thousand clicks costs £11,410 — a ninth of one project. The firms bidding that price have already done this arithmetic.

Three cautions travel with it:

  • £100,000 is Paul's figure, given on 12 August 2026 as "100k". It is read here as sterling, and whether it means fee or total contract value has not been confirmed. Both change the sums.
  • The two rates that turn visits into clients — how many visitors enquire, and how many enquiries close — have never been measured for this firm. Every projection above assumes them.
  • The website has to work before any of it applies. The security certificate is broken and three of the four service pages still carry placeholder Latin. At £100,000 a project, a referred buyer who bounces off a browser warning is not a housekeeping problem.

What is still open

Three decisions closed on 12 August 2026. Paul confirmed that tenant representation, property and asset management, and valuation and survey are all part of the portfolio, and that the firm wants enquiries on all three. That releases 10,540 searches a month across Britain, America and Australia, and 3,770 a month worldwide in the Hong Kong tree.

What his answer does not settle:

  • Formal valuation is licensed work, in Britain and in Australia. A Red Book valuation may only be signed by an RICS Registered Valuer; an Australian valuation by a Certified Practising Valuer. The pages can be written as advisory. They cannot offer the signed product unless the firm has a registered valuer or a named partner firm. This is the one place where a yes from the client is not enough.
  • America's D8 and D9 are different questions and stay open — whether the firm is entering the American market at all, and which address goes on the contact page.
  • What may be published from the work product, and under whose name — decision D10, covering the Covent Garden report, the Mall of Berlin brief and the book.

Beyond those:

  • ~~Four British terms carrying 2,470 searches a month have no results-page reading.~~ All four were read on 12 August 2026 and all four are rejected. Two sell letting and sales through a local branch; two sell running buildings — rent, service charges, maintenance — and one of those two has job adverts on the page. The 2,470 searches a month are not this firm's business. See the British tab.
  • Ten further English-speaking markets have a demand number and nothing else: Canada 1,330 a month, Ireland 1,140 (1,000 of it a single phrase, possibly a data artefact), the United Arab Emirates 870, New Zealand 540, South Africa 490, Singapore 400, Malaysia 390, the Philippines 360. India shows 1,570 and is almost certainly worthless here, because an Indian "retail broker" is a stockbroker. None of these may be costed or prototyped.
  • commercial property management was rejected in the Hong Kong research on the grounds that the firm does not sell it. Paul's answer voids that reason, so the term was re-measured on 12 August 2026: Hong Kong shows 20 searches a month, and the research tool holds no Hong Kong results page for it. It is now not a search target for Hong Kong — killed on demand, not on fit. The results page itself is still unread, and at 20 searches a month reading it would change nothing.
  • Whether Husband Retail can actually serve any of these markets is unexamined. Every number here measures demand, not the firm's ability to answer the phone.
  • The buyer check has landed, and it disagrees with this plan. It is the Fit audit tab. Its central objection — that the firm does not sell what these searchers want — is largely answered by Paul's confirmation above. Its second objection stands: no buyer has been interviewed.

A second opinion on the four market tabs, run 12 August 2026. It asks one question the results-page check cannot answer: are the people typing these words looking for what Husband Retail actually sells? It is an internal adversarial review built from Digital Movement's own files — the client dossier, the positioning brief, the sales map, the ABM list and this document. No buyer was interviewed. Nothing in the market tabs has been changed on the strength of it. Read it as a challenge to the plan, not as a replacement for it.

Paul's answer, 12 August 2026 — the audit's central premise has changed

The offer test below refuted all twelve surviving terms on the grounds that Husband Retail does not sell tenant representation, property and asset management, or valuation. Paul confirmed today that all three are part of the portfolio, and that the firm wants enquiries on them.

That does not make the audit wrong about everything. The buyer test and the conversion test stand on their own feet, and it remains true that no buyer has been interviewed. But the largest single reason terms were killed here was an offer definition taken from the dossier, and the dossier was out of date. Read §3 and §4 with that in mind.

He also priced the outcome: one new project is worth about £100,000. The audit's argument that search cannot be an acquisition channel rested partly on the volumes being small. At £100,000 a project, a handful of clients a year is not a small result.

1. The question, and the answer

The four market tabs certify 112 search terms as carrying buying intent. That verdict was read off a live Google results page and it means one thing: somebody is selling something on this page, so a service page could win here.

This audit asked a harder question, in three parts:

  1. Does Husband Retail sell the thing the ranking firms are selling?
  2. Is the person typing it on the buyer list — the owner-side names in the 4,879-person ABM file?
  3. Would the enquiry come to Husband Retail, or to Savills, CBRE, JLL or a licensed local firm?

Twelve of the 112 passed the first pass. Then three adversarial reviewers, one per test, were asked to refute those twelve. The offer test refuted 12 of 12. The buyer test refuted 12 of 12. The conversion test refuted 11 of 12. Every surviving term was refuted at least twice.

On this reading, none of the 112 confirmed terms is clean demand from someone looking for what Paul does. That is a strong claim and it rests on documents, not on buyers, which is the weakness it names about itself in §7.

2. The scoreboard

A term is ADJACENT when the searcher has a real commercial-property problem and the answer is a service Husband Retail does not sell. It is WRONG BUSINESS when the searcher is not a property buyer at all.

Two warnings travel with that table. The Hong Kong row is worldwide volume, not Hong Kong volume — inside Hong Kong the whole 42-term set is a few hundred searches a month, and the six as-filed matches are worth roughly 50 searches a month in Hong Kong itself. And the term counts are higher than the page counts in each market tab because they include secondary phrases and, for Hong Kong, the wider term register.

3. What the firm sells, and who pays

The audit re-read the dossier and the positioning brief before judging anything.

Four written pillars. Development consultancy — market analysis, retail concept development, layout analysis, retail-mix planning, all owner-side and before a scheme is built. Leasing strategy and execution for the landlord, with a hard rule attached: "We only handle leasing for developments we have worked on." Positioning and repositioning strategy for existing centres. Investment advisory and asset management — the newest pillar, added 2020, whose only named leader has since left the firm.

One buyer side, stated as pitch-critical. "Husband's paying clients are the landlord/sell-side — developers, landlords, investors. Luxury brands are the tenants they recruit." The ABM list has the same shape: Majid Al Futtaim, Dubai Holding, Unibail-Rodamco-Westfield, Diriyah, Irvine Company, Oxford Properties, Value Retail, Emaar, Simon Property Group, QuadReal, Central Pattana.

Things the evidence says the firm does not sell — and this is where most of the 112 terms land: tenant-representation broking (explicitly disclaimed in the dossier, and contradicted by Paul's own track record for Dean & DeLuca, Ralph Lauren and Chrome Hearts — that contradiction is decision D7); day-to-day building and centre management; Red Book or bank-grade valuation; town planning and statutory consenting; and general commercial agency or unit letting.

How work arrives today. Paul's network, the book, the ICSC circuit and warm referral. The three live 2026 engagements — the AI report for Covent Garden's owner, the Mall of Berlin due-diligence brief, and the Hong Kong equity conversation — are all outbound or relationship-led. None came from search. The site has 477 links coming in from 142 different websites, but only 16 search terms it ranks for and about 11 unpaid visits a month. People recommend Husband Retail constantly and nothing catches the traffic.

4. The three lenses, and what each one killed

Offer — 12 of 12 refuted. Every one of the twelve results pages is owned by firms selling from a much wider shelf: agency on any asset, centre management, Red Book valuation, appraisal-grade feasibility, tax, debt placement, engineering and consenting. An enquiry from those pages would have to be subcontracted, referred out, or answered by a licensed professional the firm does not employ.

Buyer — 12 of 12 refuted. The sharpest finding here: volume and results page were in different countries. Four of the Hong Kong wins — retail advisory, retail advisory services, retail consultancy, retail consultancy services — have Hong Kong volume of 0, 0, 30 and 0. The searchers are in America and Britain, and the British results page for those exact phrases is Bain, Retail Economics, Q5 and a recruiter. A landlord-flavoured Hong Kong page was read for a phrase almost nobody in Hong Kong types, and the buyer was then declared a landlord.

Conversion — 11 of 12 refuted. Outside Hong Kong the firm is a founder-led boutique with no office, no local entity, no licence, no local comparables and no publishable case study in the market where the searcher is sitting. The thing that has won every engagement in the file — prior knowledge of Paul — is exactly what a stranger arriving from Google does not have.

5. Ten terms that may have been rejected wrongly

The audit ran the check in both directions. These were killed in the market tabs and deserve a second look. None of them is cleared for use — each still needs the full three-part check before it could back a page.

The casualties are systematically the Asia-side and landlord-side vocabulary — repositioning strategy, tenant coordination, mall leasing, retail portfolio management, commercial real estate hong kong

6. One correction the audit makes to the pillar page

Hong Kong's pillar is built on retail consulting — the American spelling, whose results page is management consultancies and job boards. The British and Hong Kong spelling, retail consultancy, returns Colliers Hong Kong, Knight Frank Hong Kong and a direct boutique peer, and it is currently filed as a supporting phrase rather than a page.

And the phrase Savills ranks first for in Hong Kong is retail landlord advisory — a page titled exactly that. It describes Husband Retail's position better than anything in the tree, and it has no page at all.

7. The biggest untested assumption

Stated by the audit in its own words, and it is the honest headline of this tab:

That the person who hires Husband Retail searches at all.

Seven research passes, 243 terms measured, 108 live results pages read, four market trees, three continents — and not one human buyer has been asked a single question. The programme measures supply and infers demand from it. Every rejection has the same shape: the results page is recruiters, therefore the searcher is a recruiter. That inference runs backwards. A results page tells you what Google chose to serve. It does not tell you who typed the query, and it is silent on whether the buyer typed anything.

The test costs almost nothing and has never been run. The positioning brief already carries the question, starred and unanswered: "Where did your last five jobs come from? Name the route for each one." Five calls to people already on the ABM list — "when you last appointed a retail consultant, what did you actually do?" — would produce a better model of this market than 243 rows from the research tool.

Seventeen further gaps are recorded in the audit output, all of them questions only the client can answer: which pillar earns the money, how the firm charges, what a typical job is worth, how many projects it can run at once, who delivers the investment pillar now, how long the sales cycle is, and who else is in the room at the pitch.

8. What this tab does and does not change

It does not change the market tabs. Every volume, competition score and results-page reading in this document stands. The Commercial? column still means what it says — a live results page with sellers on it — and it has not been touched.

It does change what the trees are for. On this reading, search for Husband Retail is a credibility layer, not an acquisition channel: pages that hold up when a referred buyer looks the firm up, that support named-account outbound, and that get cited in AI answers. The Australian tab in particular is argued down from twenty-one pages to about two plus the insights format.

It sharpened three decisions, and Paul then answered all three the other way. The audit argued from the documents that D8 (property and asset management) and D9 (valuation) both resolved to no, because no written pillar covers running a building and the only RICS-credentialled person in the dossier has left. On 12 August 2026 Paul confirmed that all three — including D7, tenant representation — are part of the portfolio and that the firm wants the enquiries. The documents were out of date, and the audit inherited them. What survives untouched is the licensing point, which is not a matter of opinion: a Red Book valuation still requires an RICS Registered Valuer.

It is a second opinion, not a measurement. It was produced by ten reviewers reading the same files, with three of them briefed to attack the conclusions. It has no buyer research behind it, and it says so itself. The next move it argues for is five phone calls, not more search terms.

1. The short answer

Australia first. The United Kingdom second.

Australia is not the biggest market on the list — the UK is — but Australia is the one where the word retail still means retail property. In the UK it does not. Every UK phrase with "retail" in it turned out to belong to somebody else: recruitment agencies, or property listing portals. The UK is still worth opening, but as a commercial property market, not a retail one.

Australia is also easier. Its best terms score 8 to 17 out of 100 for competition. The UK's score 24 to 34.

2. How the markets rank by demand

Twenty-four search terms — the services Husband Retail sells — added up per country. Three terms were left out of the totals on purpose: retail analytics, retail space and retail consulting, because the US pass already proved those belong to software firms, listing portals and management consultants.

"Not researched" means exactly that. There is a demand number and nothing else — no competition score, no idea whether those searchers are buyers. None of those markets may be costed, prototyped or written for until the other two checks are done. Ireland's number in particular should not be trusted at face value: 88% of it is one phrase.

5. What the two markets mean side by side

The UK has more demand. Australia has better demand.

United Kingdom Australia
Total demand, 24 service terms3,900/mo2,710/mo
Terms that survived the results-page check2 clear, 2 mixed3 clear, 1 mixed
Demand behind the clear terms1,770/mo1,380/mo
Competition of those terms24 and 348, 8 and 17
Does "retail" work as a word?No — it means recruitment or listingsYes — retail property management is clean at competition 8
Highest advertiser spend seen£11.41 (tenant representation)A$10.42 (commercial real estate consulting)

Australia's three clear terms are all easier than the UK's easiest. A new site can realistically rank for a competition-8 term. Competition 34 in a market full of Savills and Lambert Smith Hampton is a much longer job.

6. Why India's 1,570 should be ignored

India ranks fourth on raw demand and is almost certainly worthless here.

  • retail broker — 390/mo. In Indian search, a "retail broker" is a stock broker serving retail investors. It is a financial services term, not property.
  • retail analytics — 2,900/mo (already excluded from the totals). India is a major offshore hub for retail software and analytics work. This is a software and jobs term.
  • mall management — 210/mo and shop leasing — 170/mo are plausible, but neither has been checked.

No Indian results page was read. Until one is, India is not a candidate.

7. The markets nobody has checked yet, and why two of them are interesting

Nothing below has a competition score or a results-page reading. These are leads, not findings.

  • United Arab Emirates — 870/mo. Commercial property management alone is 480/mo, and mall management shows up at 40/mo, which it does almost nowhere else. Dubai and Abu Dhabi have a large, young, professionally managed retail property sector. Worth checking properly.
  • Ireland — 1,140/mo, but 1,000 of that is one phrase (commercial property management). For a country of five million people that is a strikingly high number and may be a data artefact. It needs verifying before anyone gets excited about it.
  • Canada — 1,330/mo across eleven terms. The most evenly spread of the unchecked markets, which usually means a real industry rather than one odd spike. But Canadian results pages are heavily influenced by American sites, so the check on what the searcher wants matters more here than usual.
  • Singapore — 400/mo. The smallest number worth mentioning, and arguably the best strategic fit: same time zone as Hong Kong, same business culture, overlapping landlord and brand clients, English-speaking, no new legal entity needed for most advisory work. Low volume, high value per enquiry.
  • New Zealand (540), South Africa (490), Malaysia (390), Philippines (360). Real but small. Not worth opening before the bigger ones are running.

8. What we recommend

  1. Australia. Three clean terms, competition 8 to 17, advertisers paying A$4.50 to A$10.40 a click. Best ratio of opportunity to effort on the list.
  2. United Kingdom. Bigger market, harder pages, and it must be built as a commercial property offer. The word "retail" cannot lead.
  3. United Arab Emirates or Singapore, once one of the above is live. UAE for volume, Singapore for fit.
  4. Canada and Ireland need their numbers verified before they are discussed with the client.

9. Two things to settle before any of this is costed

  • Tenant representation, again. It is Australia's cleanest results page and one of its two easiest terms, and the same problem applies as in Hong Kong and the United States: the client dossier says Husband Retail is not a tenant-rep broker. Three markets now have this as a top opportunity. It needs a decision from Paul rather than three separate holds.
  • Whether Husband Retail can service these markets at all. Every number here measures demand, not the client's ability to serve it. A page ranking in Perth is worth nothing without someone who can take the call.

10. What this document does not contain

  • No competition score or results-page readings for any market except the UK and Australia. Ten markets have demand numbers only.
  • No page tree for either verified market. That is the next step, and it needs the same treatment the US tree got: every term read individually, secondaries separated, gate run.
  • No China. That market was tested to destruction on 12 August 2026 and the finding — that it cannot be measured with the tools we have — is written up in the China tab of 00-sitemap.md.
  • No estimates. Every figure above came out of a tool or off a live results page on 12 August 2026, and anything missing is marked missing.

Sorting and filtering the tree. In the HTML version there is a box above the tree for each column: pick a value and the tree narrows to it, pick values in two boxes and it narrows to both. A count under the boxes says how many of the 36 rows you are looking at, and Clear filters puts them all back. Click any heading to re-order the tree — by volume, by competition, by whether the results page has been read, or by build state. Click again to reverse it, a third time to put it back.

Reading the Commercial? column. Yes means the live results page has pages on it that sell something, so a service page can win there — that covers the terms read as Commercial and the ones read as Mixed. No means the results page is definitions and explainers only; those pages are worth building for authority and for AI answers, but they will not bring in enquiries on their own. A dash means the page has no search term at all, so the question does not apply. The verdict is derived from the column beside it that records what the searcher wants, which is a live results page read — it is not a separate measurement and nothing here is guessed.

Checked on the live results page: 22 of 22. Every page in this tree that carries a search term has had its live Google results page read as a searcher in Hong Kong sees it, twenty results, with personal tailoring off, on the date in the column — twenty on 11 August 2026, real estate feasibility study on 12 August, and retail consulting on 10 August. There are no unverified terms in the tree; that was the entry condition, not a target. The fourteen remaining rows have no term to verify, which is a different thing from a failed check and is written out in words rather than left blank.

Commercial: 9 of the 22. Nine results pages have pages on them that sell something, so nine of these pages can bring in enquiries directly — the five service pages, the homepage, /landlord-representation, the new /feasibility-studies, and retail analytics, where CBRE Hong Kong holds the first place. The other thirteen are definitions and explainers: they are how the site earns the right to rank at all, and how it gets quoted in AI answers, but the enquiry comes later, through a link across to one of the nine.

Total measured demand behind the site: 88,020 searches a month worldwide. On the terms where a Hong Kong figure was recorded, about 1,160 a month sit inside Hong Kong — and 970 of that is two terms, gross leasable area (880) and turnover rent (90).

How to read the three states

There are three, never two, because a blank cell reads as a zero and "we looked and found nothing" must never look like "we have not looked."

  • A term with numbers — volume, competition and a dated results-page read. It may back a page.
  • Not a search target — measured, and there is nothing there. Case studies, contact, legal. The page still ships; it carries no search term.
  • Not researched — would appear as pending with a note saying what is missing. There are none in this tree. Four terms sit in that state — tenant mix (now settled as a permanent block, see D6), types of retail, retail mix and what is retail consulting, all three of the last still waiting on a results-page read — and that is exactly why none of them appears above.

A further forty terms were measured on 11 August 2026, all eighteen open results pages were read, and the tree still does not change. A fourth research pass went looking for more terms that could bring in enquiries and for questions people ask an AI. Every candidate was then put through three tests, not one: is anyone selling on that results page, is the thing being sold a thing Husband Retail sells, and is the person searching a landlord or a student. Fifteen of the eighteen failed. Lease renewal is residential leasehold and government forms. Net lettable area is Australian surveyors. Catchment area analysis is mapping software. Shopping centre design is Pinterest and ArchDaily. How to increase footfall is advice for one shopkeeper. Types of retail and retail mix are coursework, as suspected. Full verdicts in §10 of the research document.

One real decision came out of it, and it is D7 below. Tenant representation has 2,610 searches a month at competition 4 — the cheapest ranking anywhere on this project — and Savills Hong Kong holds the number one result with a page called exactly Retail Tenant Representation. It passes every mechanical test and fails a positioning one: the dossier says the firm is "not a tenant-rep broker". Paul has to settle it, and nothing is built until he does.

A fifth pass on 11 August 2026 measured forty more, and those are not in the tree either — none of them has been read yet. It went at five angles the earlier passes never tried: the sectors the firm actually works in, the four service pillars in their own words, the deal side, the trade's own terms of art, and publishing. Fifteen have real demand. Three are worth reading first: leasing strategy (540 a month at competition 1 — the lowest score on this project, and the exact name of the firm's second pillar), luxury retail consulting (230 at competition 13, where a named boutique peer already ranks), and travel retail (4,000, and 170 of them in Hong Kong — the second-best Hong Kong number found anywhere on this project). Google blocked again before any of them could be read, so all fifteen are "not researched" and none may be built, drafted or costed. The queue and the reasoning are in §11 of the research document.

Two of that pass's findings are worth reading even though they produced no pages. The firm's own vocabulary is not the market's, anywhere: retail masterplanning and shopping centre repositioning are how Husband Retail describes two of its four pillars, and neither phrase is searched at all — the same wall retailer representation hit in D7. And the biggest number in the whole project is the most useless one: outlet mall gets 295,680 searches a month, and they are shoppers looking for somewhere to go.

On the question terms the answer came back clean: they are headings, not pages. Sixteen were measured and every one with usable demand turned out to be the question form of a term already in the tree — what is an anchor tenant at 290 against anchor tenant at 2,450, and its results page even carries the same University of Hong Kong paper. Building both would put two of our own pages in front of one reader. Instead each question becomes a heading inside the page that already owns the topic, with the answer in the first two sentences underneath, which is the shape an AI answer engine quotes. One exception: do not use what is a trade area as a heading — its results page is half mapping software and half Wikipedia's article on free trade areas, which is a different subject.

The sixth pass — one word we had been missing

A sixth pass on 11 August 2026 measured ninety-eight more terms and read fifteen live results pages, and for the first time in three passes the tree changes.

/landlord-representation is the new page. Landlord representation has 850 searches a month at competition 1, advertisers pay $4.59 a click for it, and Savills Hong Kong holds the number one result. It is the exact mirror of the term stuck in D7: tenant representation describes work the dossier says Husband Retail does not do, and landlord representation describes the side the dossier says its paying clients are on. It also answers D7 by going round it — the firm can own the landlord phrase without settling the retailer question at all.

One condition on that page. Nine of the thirteen results are "What is landlord representation?" explainers, so the page has to teach the term before it sells the service. A pure service page will not hold this results page.

A second term cleared and did not earn a page. Retail advisory services (410 a month, competition 18, $5.68 a click) has the cleanest results page found anywhere on this project — Savills Hong Kong's Retail Landlord Advisory Services at number one, then CBRE, Colliers Hong Kong, JLL, Bain and a Retail consulting firms in Hong Kong directory. Nine of thirteen sell exactly this. It is the same reader as /retail-consulting, so it rides along inside that page rather than competing with it — but the phrase Savills wins on, retail landlord advisory, describes Husband Retail better than "retail consulting" does, and the pillar's opening line should say so.

Thirteen of the fifteen were turned down, and eleven of those failed on who is searching, not on how hard the term is. Travel retail — the biggest Hong Kong number on the fifth-pass list — is job adverts and duty-free operators. Property asset management is a job title. Retail transformation is software. Lease advisory belongs to a British government service for residential leaseholders. Retail strategy is coursework and shopkeeper software. Retail property investment is private investors buying a single unit. Integrated resort is tourists. Restaurant leasing is Hong Kong listings sites. Pop up store draws 1,000 searches a month in Hong Kong and they are people looking for a pop-up to visit — though Link REIT holds the top result with a landlord-side page, which is worth copying inside /insights/pop-up-retail. And luxury retail consulting has BCG, Bain and Simon-Kucher on it, selling brand strategy to luxury brands — a healthy page for work the dossier says the firm does not do.

Leasing strategy — 540 a month at competition 1, and the name of the firm's own second service pillar — was demoted rather than blocked. Half its results page is lease accounting: accounting software, a CPA exam question, Thomson Reuters on finance leases. The other half is CBRE Hong Kong and Colliers Hong Kong. It becomes a secondary phrase on /retail-leasing, where the page title can make clear which kind of leasing is meant.

One finding is not a page and matters more than most of them. Retail market report returns eight Hong Kong market reports in nine results — Knight Frank, Savills, JLL, Cushman & Wakefield, HKTDC, and the government's own statistics office. That is the competitor set's front door in this market, and it is a quarterly publishing commitment, not a page in a tree. Flagged for the content plan.

The same pass killed the city-name idea outright. Retail consultant hong kong, property consultant hong kong and retail leasing hong kong have zero searches between them. Nobody types the city after the service. Hong Kong reaches these pages through the plain term and through what is written inside them, or it does not reach them at all.

Thirty-four terms still sit in not researched — a volume and a competition each, no results page. All of them are listed in §6 below with their numbers, so nothing is lost and nothing looks cleared that isn't.

The seventh pass — the twenty are in

A seventh pass on 12 August 2026 measured fifty more terms and read twenty-two more results pages, and it finished the job the client set: twenty search terms that are commercially relevant and checked on a live Google results page. Eight of the twenty came out of this pass. One is a new page; seven ride inside pages this tree already has. The full twenty are listed at the end of this section.

The find is /feasibility-studies, backed by real estate feasibility study — 670 searches a month at competition 8, $4.07 a click, with Altus Group, Marsh & Partners and Savills Hong Kong all selling feasibility work on the page. Savills' own page is called Real Estate Feasibility Studies & Development Consultancy, which is the exact pairing Husband Retail does under the name masterplanning. It is the cheapest fee-earning page found on this project after landlord representation.

The other eight that cleared do not need pages. Retail consultancy (2,640 a month) is the strongest of them, because Colliers Hong Kong, Knight Frank Hong Kong and Waterhouse Wade Hong Kong all rank on it — that is Husband Retail's own competitor set on one Hong Kong results page. Leasing services (2,460) puts Colliers, Savills and CBRE Hong Kong in the top three. Property advisory (650) and property advisory services (310) add Savills Hong Kong, Colliers Hong Kong, PwC Hong Kong, WSP and Knight Frank Australia. Commercial real estate consulting (1,080 at competition 15) adds Avison Young and Lambert Smith Hampton. Lease restructuring (310 at competition 4) has JLL at number one selling it as a service. Mall redevelopment (180 at competition 6) has Cushman & Wakefield ranking with a page called Mall Repositioning — Husband Retail's own service, under Husband Retail's own word. That last one is thin and is written down as thin: eight results, no paid adverts, and no Hong Kong searches at all.

Fourteen terms were read and turned down, and the pattern held for the third pass running: they failed on who is searching, not on how hard they are. Retail brokerage (1,760) is stock broking — SGX, StashAway, Chief Securities. Landlord services (2,080) is residential lettings. Tenant retention (740) is residential lettings software. Asset management strategy (1,540) is equipment-lifecycle software and council asset plans. Real estate consultancy (8,630) and property consultancy (7,410) are job boards and Hong Kong luxury-home agencies. Commercial leasing (5,820) and retail space leasing (1,240) are tenants hunting premises. Value add real estate (840, and $14.41 a click — the most expensive term on this project) is property funds raising capital.

One term was read and held rather than rejected: mall valuation (420 at competition 3). The sellers on that page are valuers — Newmark, Lotus Amity, Cornerstone — and the buyer is a mall owner, which is the right reader. The open question was whether Husband Retail signs off valuation as one of its services. Paul answered yes on 12 August 2026, so this is a cheap page and it is cleared — written as advisory, because a formal valuation is licensed work.

One question the seventh pass settled for good: rent review had been left on hold since the fourth pass. Its commercial twin commercial rent review (640, competition 3) reads as UK and Irish legal guidance plus the RICS surveyor directory, with no Hong Kong demand at all. Both are now closed.

18lease restructuring3104Secondary — /insights/commercial-lease-negotiation
19mall redevelopment1806Secondary — /insights/retail-development
20retail market report85035A quarterly publication, not a page

Number twenty needs a word of explanation, because it is the one that is not a page. Retail market report draws 850 searches a month, costs $7.35 a click and carries three blocks of paid adverts — the strongest buying signal on this project. Eight of the nine results are Hong Kong retail market reports published by Knight Frank Hong Kong, Cushman & Wakefield, Savills Hong Kong Research and JLL. That is Husband Retail's competitor set doing something Husband Retail could do, for Husband Retail's buyers. It clears all three tests. It is filed as a publication rather than a page only because the right answer to that search is a report with a date on it, refreshed every quarter — a static page would lose to a fresh one every time.

One term was measured, read, and left open on purpose. Mall valuation (420 at competition 3) has the right buyer and real sellers, but every seller is a valuer. Whether Husband Retail offers valuation is a question for Paul, not for research. It is not counted in the twenty.

What is still unread. Twenty-seven measured terms have a volume and a competition but no results page reading, so nothing may be built, drafted or costed against any of them. They are all listed with their numbers in §6. Reading them is paused because Google rate-limited this machine on 12 August, and both paid stand-ins — Serper and Firecrawl — are out of credit and were not topped up, because nothing gets bought without sign-off.

2. What changed from v2.0, and why

v2.0 had four pages with a search term behind them. That was the honest answer to the question it had asked: which of Husband Retail's services can carry a page of their own. Only service phrasings had been measured, and almost all of them failed.

The question was too narrow. A twenty-page inbound programme is not twenty service pages — it is a handful of pages that sell and a much larger body of pages that teach. Those are informational terms, and none had been tested.

So sixty more terms were measured. Sixteen cleared, and they split the way an inbound programme is supposed to split:

  • 6 fee-earning pages — someone is trying to hire a firm. Three are new: retail property management, retail site selection, retail expansion strategy.
  • 14 reputation pages — someone is trying to understand something. Thirteen are new, and they carry 75,290 of the 86,500 searches. The six fee-earning pages carry the other 11,210.

The find of the project is retail property management. Its results page is Savills Hong Kong, Knight Frank Hong Kong, Colliers Hong Kong, CBRE Hong Kong, JLL, Kerry Properties and Swire — the client's competitor set, in the client's own city — and its competition is 20, against 36 for the pillar.

Forty-four terms were measured and rejected, and the reasons are worth keeping: jobs boards, dictionary entries, software vendors, consumer shopping guides, and four terms that belong to a different industry altogether. footfall analysis looked perfect at competition 1 and turned out to be structural engineering — vibration in buildings. Full register in section 7 of the research document.

3. Build order

Wave 1 — the pages that earn (6).

  1. / — re-target at retail real estate, add the definitional section the results page demands.
  2. /retail-consulting — built; finalise the headings, work the secondaries in.
  3. /retail-property-management — the new fee-earning page. Highest priority of the three new ones: right competitor set, competition 20.
  4. /retail-leasing — landlord-side on every heading.
  5. /retail-site-selection — cost per click $5.72, and $13.78 on its secondary.
  6. /retail-expansion-strategy — competition 2. The cheapest commercial page on the site.

Plus /contact, /the-firm, /work and the eight project pages — the proof and conversion layer the six point at.

Wave 2 — authority, cheapest first (7).

In order of competition, easiest first, because early wins fund the hard ones: trade area analysis (5) · turnover rent (6) · retail development (6) · gross leasable area (9) · occupancy cost (9) · anchor tenant (12) · retail analytics (15).

gross leasable area is the one to watch: 880 searches a month inside Hong Kong, at competition 9.

Wave 3 — authority, the harder half (7).

highest and best use (19) · commercial lease negotiation (19) · percentage rent (22) · experiential retail (34) · pop up retail (34) · mixed use development (46) · retail trends (58).

retail trends is an annual outlook piece and needs Husband Retail's own data to beat Savills Hong Kong. It is the last page to write, not the first.

Then /privacy, /terms.

4. Decisions

D1 — the homepage and the pillar were chasing the same term. Resolved. The current husband-retail.com ranks #7 for retail consulting, which is what the new pillar targets. The pillar keeps it. The homepage takes retail real estate — a different results page, lower competition, and already what its title tag says the firm is. There is no separate /retail-real-estate page; it would be a thin near-duplicate. Reverse it by giving the homepage no term and adding that page back. Still open with the client.

D2 — the eight project pages. Kept as /work/... children. They are the proof behind the pillar. Open question for the client: are all eight publishable, and under whose name? Still open with the client.

D3 — /insights is now a real section. Resolved. Fourteen articles, not one. It gets an index page and a topic grouping.

D4 — turnover rent and percentage rent are two pages. They are the same commercial idea under two names, which normally means one page. Here the two results pages share no domain: turnover rent is British, Hong Kong and Australian; percentage rent is American. Two results pages, two audiences, two pages. If they later start returning the same domains, merge them and redirect the weaker one.

D5 — retail analytics ships with a condition. Seven of nine results are software vendors. It clears only because CBRE Hong Kong holds #1 with a property service. The page must be about retail property analytics — footfall, catchment, trade area, tenant performance. If the draft drifts toward software, kill the page rather than publish it.

D6 — tenant mix is blocked, permanently, and it hurts. Best results page of the whole project — Savills Hong Kong, a University of Hong Kong paper on tenant mix in Hong Kong shopping centres, FNRP, CRE-360 — and 960 searches a month. But the research tool returns no competition score and no guess at what the searcher wants, on any phrasing, in any of the tool's country records. Two of three inputs missing, so no page. It becomes a section of /insights/anchor-tenant.

Re-measured 11 August 2026, and the reason is now understood: the tool has no American volume for this term at all. Brazil 170 and the UK 40 are the biggest markets. Competition, cost per click and the tool's guess at what the searcher wants are all computed off the tool's US records, so a term Americans do not search cannot produce them. Re-measuring will not change that — this is a permanent block, not a pending one. The variants retail tenant mix (20 a month) and mall tenant mix (80) are below the measurement floor as well.

Paul's answer, 12 August 2026 — D7 is settled: yes

Husband Retail does represent retailers, and wants the enquiries. Option 1 in the table below — build it, written landlord-aware — is the live plan. Three terms come off hold: tenant representation (2,610 worldwide, competition 4), retail tenant representation (370, competition 16) and occupier services (790, competition 16) — 3,770 searches a month worldwide.

Keep the Hong Kong caveat in view. Inside Hong Kong those three are worth about 50 searches a month between them. The volume is in America and Australia, which is an argument for those trees carrying this term hardest, not this one.

/landlord-representation still gets built. Both sides of the table are now honest.

D7 — does Husband Retail represent retailers? (settled 12 August 2026 — yes)

The cheapest ranking on this project is sitting on the wrong side of the table. Tenant representation has 2,610 searches a month at competition 4; the qualified retail tenant representation has 370 at competition 16. Both results pages were read on 11 August 2026. Savills Hong Kong holds the number one result with a page called exactly Retail Tenant Representation, JLL is there too, and three advertisers are paying for the click. Real demand, real buyers, and a competition score close to nothing.

The problem is not the search term. It is what the page would say we are. The dossier's own first line: Husband Retail is "NOT a retail brand, store operator, tenant-rep broker, or brand-strategy firm", and the paying clients are developers, landlords and investors. A tenant-representation page advertises the category the firm defined itself against.

And yet the same dossier records that Paul "has led retailer representation assignments in Asia Pacific on behalf of Dean & DeLuca, Ralph Lauren and Chrome Hearts." The work exists, with names worth printing. It is played down, not absent.

There is no way to say it in the firm's own words. Retailer representation has zero searches worldwide. So do retail brand expansion and retail expansion consultant. It is the market's word or nothing.

Option What happens
1. Build it, qualified (recommended)/retail-tenant-representation, written landlord-aware: we act for brands entering Asia, and we know what the landlord will say because we sit on that side of the table most days. Dean & DeLuca, Ralph Lauren and Chrome Hearts are the proof. Needs Paul to accept the label on his own site.
2. Make it a sectionGoes inside /retail-leasing. Positioning stays clean, none of the 2,610 is won.
3. Leave itConsistent with the dossier. Savills keeps the term.

Paul signed this off on 12 August 2026: the firm does represent retailers, and wants the enquiries. Option 1 is the live plan. It was a positioning decision, not a search-visibility one, and it was the only thing the fourth research pass produced that changed anything.

The sixth pass found a fourth option that does not need his permission. Landlord representation — the mirror image of the term above — has 850 searches a month at competition 1, costs $4.59 a click, and Savills Hong Kong holds the number one result on it as well. It describes the side the dossier says the paying clients are on, so it can be built today, and it is now /landlord-representation in the tree. That does not settle D7; it removes the pressure to settle it quickly. If Paul declines the retailer label, the firm still owns a representation page — the right one.

5. Rules this tree has to keep

  1. One term, one page. Six near-variants ride as secondaries: retail consulting services, retail consulting firms and retail strategy consulting on the pillar; site selection analysis and retail location strategy on /retail-site-selection; catchment area analysis on /insights/trade-area-analysis; retail lease on /retail-leasing; shopping mall trends on /insights/retail-trends.
  2. A service with no search term becomes a section, not a page.
  3. Nothing ships against a term that has not been read on a live results page. The research tool's guess at what the searcher wants has now been wrong six times on this project — it calls retail consultant "Informational" when it is a job search, and lease incentive "Commercial" when the whole results page is IFRS 16 accounting.
  4. A page with no term says so, in words, on the page tree.
  5. Every search-visibility page in this tree is backed by a search term. If a row cannot show a volume, a competition and a results-page date, it is not a search-visibility page.
  6. A term missing one input is blocked, however good it looks. tenant mix is the standing example.

Digital Movement · Husband Retail · 11 August 2026. Detail: 01-seo-page-guide.md. Headings, editable: copy-review/seo-sitemap-extended.html. Gate: python3 "Research/SEO Page QA Checklist/sitemap-tree-gate.py" "Husband Retail/Website/00-sitemap.md"

6. The search terms people actually type

Seven research passes, 243 terms measured, on 11 and 12 August 2026 in one logged-in the research tool browser session. 210 of them are typed by real people — together, 943,960 searches a month worldwide. Every one is below with its numbers and its state. Sort any column; filter any column.

This is the evidence file, not the plan. The plan is the tree at the top of this page. A row here can carry a huge number and still be worthless: outlet mall sits at the top of the table with 295,680 searches a month, and they are people looking for somewhere to shop.

Read the last two columns together. Results page read says whether a human opened the live Google results for that exact term and looked at who was on it. One hundred and eight have been read. Seven are variants of a term that was read — same subject, same results page, so they were judged with it. Ninety-five have not been read at all. An unread term cannot back a page however good its numbers look, which is why twenty-seven of them say Not researched instead of appearing in the tree.

What the states mean:

  • Page — it backs a page in the tree above, and the page is named.
  • Secondary search term — same subject as a page we already have, so it rides along inside that page instead of splitting the ranking between two of ours.
  • Section — it becomes a heading inside a page, usually because it is the question form of something we already own.
  • Decision D7 — three terms that were waiting on Paul. Answered yes on 12 August 2026; all three are cleared. See §4.
  • Rejected — read on the live results page and turned down, with the reason in the cell. Almost all of these failed on who is searching, not on how hard the term is.
  • Held — read, not turned down, not usable yet.
  • Not a page — real demand, right reader, but the answer is a publication or a section rather than a page in a tree.
  • Blocked — no US data — the research tool computes competition and the searcher's purpose from its American records only, so a term Americans do not type comes back missing two of the three required numbers. That is permanent, not a delay.
  • Blocked — below the floor — under 150 searches a month worldwide.
  • Not researched — volume and competition in hand, no results page read. Nothing may be built, drafted or costed against these.

A further 33 terms came back with no measurable demand at all, so there is nothing to record and they are not in the table: retailer representation, retail masterplanning, retail masterplan, shopping centre repositioning, shopping centre asset enhancement, shopping centre tenant mix, shopping centre feasibility study, shopping centre valuation, retail consultant hong kong, property consultant hong kong, retail leasing hong kong, retail expansion asia, asia retail market, shopping mall operations, retail leasing process, retail property advisory, retail real estate advisory, retail space planning consultant, shopping center consultant, department store consulting, mixed use development consultant, store network strategy, store portfolio management, flagship store strategy, retail revitalization, shopping centre consultant, shopping mall consultant, shopping centre operations, shopping centre strategy, mall operations management, retail asset strategy, retail development consultancy and market entry hong kong.

Three things in that list are worth saying out loud. Five of them are how Husband Retail describes its own services — masterplanning, repositioning, asset enhancement, retailer representation, tenant mix. Four more are the obvious ways to name a shopping-centre adviser — shopping centre consultant, shopping mall consultant, shopping centre strategy, shopping centre operations — and every one of them is zero. Four are a Husband Retail service with the words hong kong after it. Nobody searches any of it.

So the firm's own language belongs inside the pages, never in the page titles; the city belongs in the writing, not in the search term; and the words a shopping-centre specialist would naturally reach for are the words nobody types. The terms that do carry demand are the plainer ones — retail leasing, landlord representation, retail consultancy.

1. The US page tree

28 pages · 14 carry a search term · 14 are not search targets.

Every number comes from the research tool, read in a logged-in browser, 12 August 2026 — worldwide volume, with the US share shown first. Every reading of what the searcher wants is a live Google results page read as a searcher in America sees them, twenty results, personal tailoring off, on 12 August 2026.

Slugs are written from the root, the same as Hong Kong. If the two markets ever share one domain, every US page address takes a /us/ prefix and every Hong Kong page address takes /hk/. That decision has not been made and nothing in this tree depends on it.

What the fourteen non-target pages are

They are in the tree above, with no term against them. They exist because a site needs them, not because anybody searches for them, and none should be given a search term later.

  • /about — Who the firm is, and why a Hong Kong firm is in this market
  • /team — Named people, with the US contacts first
  • /case-studies — Hub
  • /case-studies/[client-1] — One landlord assignment
  • /case-studies/[client-2] — One occupier assignment
  • /case-studies/[client-3] — One management assignment
  • /insights — Hub for the two article pages above, and everything written later
  • /contact — Phone required, email optional
  • /careers — Roles, and where they are
  • /privacy-policy — Required
  • /terms-of-use — Required
  • /accessibility — Required in several states, and cheap to do
  • /thank-you — Form confirmation, no-index
  • /404 — No-index

How to read the states

Three states, never two.

  • Backed by a search term — a term with a volume, a competition score and a live results page read on a stated date. Fourteen pages, twenty terms.
  • Not a search target — a page nobody searches for. Fourteen pages.
  • Not researched — no page in this tree is in this state. Anything added later starts here and stays here until all three numbers exist.

Checked on the live results page: 20 of 20. Every term below was read on a live Google results page on 12 August 2026, as a searcher in America sees them. Nothing here is a tool number acting alone.

Commercial: 14 of the 20. Four are mixed — the results page holds sellers and guides in roughly equal measure. Two are not commercial at all: retail market report is a publication every large firm gives away, and commercial lease negotiation is an article that happens to be read by people about to sign a lease.

Total measured demand behind the site: 20,200 searches a month worldwide, 8,860 of them in the United States.

3. What the US market turned out to be

One hundred and sixty terms were measured against the tool's US records and fifty-six live results pages were read. Four things came out of it, and three of them were not what anyone would have guessed.

The service words are tiny, and that is fine

Nothing in this market has the volume of a Hong Kong fee-earning term. retail real estate is the biggest at 1,600 a month in the US, and the twenty together are 8,860. Hong Kong's twenty are 32,090 worldwide.

That is not a weakness in the research. It is what a specialist market looks like in a country with a hundred thousand commercial agents. The searches that matter are made by a few thousand landlords and asset managers, not by the public, and the competition score show it: fourteen of the twenty sit at 20 or below, landlord representation and landlord representation services at 1.

"Retail consulting" means something completely different here

In Hong Kong, retail consulting is a fee-earning page. In the United States the first results page is Bain, BCG, McKinsey and Accenture, and the discipline being sold is merchandising and store operations for retailers — nothing to do with property. Below them sit a jobs ranking on Vault and a directory.

retail strategy does the same thing. real estate consulting firm does it too: McKinsey, Deloitte, Bain and a recruiter.

The word that works instead is commercial real estate consulting, and its first page is nine CRE consultancies with Avison Young among them. The tree uses that phrase and never the shorter one.

Everything about data belongs to software

Four terms that read as advisory work in Hong Kong turned out to be software categories in the US:

  • retail analytics — Oracle, Mastercard, ThoughtSpot, a Coursera course
  • location intelligence — Esri, CARTO, IBM, Placer.ai, Mapbox, seven paid adverts
  • retail location analysis — Esri, Placer.ai, Unacast, Cisco, seven paid adverts
  • lease abstraction — LlamaIndex, MRI, Yardi, Docugami

retail analytics is a page in the Hong Kong tree. It cannot be one here. The cost per click is $9.00 and the buyers are software buyers.

The lease glossary belongs to lawyers and dictionaries

Nine well-known retail lease terms were read — estoppel certificate, common area maintenance, snda, percentage rent, kick out clause, anchor tenant, outparcel, pad site, gross leasable area — and between them they carry 21,000 searches a month. Not one is worth a page.

Eight of the nine returned zero paid adverts. The results were Wikipedia, Cambridge Dictionary, Westlaw, Fannie Mae, Investopedia, a zoning code and a string of law firms. snda returns the Student National Dental Association and a stock ticker. kick out clause returns the residential home-sale contingency, which is not the retail co-tenancy clause at all.

The one exception is cam reconciliation — 590 a month, eight paid adverts, and a first page of property managers, accountants and a law firm all selling the work. That is the difference between a word people look up and a problem people need solved. Only the second one is worth a page.

Every one of these was measured, read on a live results page as a searcher in America sees it on 12 August 2026, and turned down. Twenty-nine failed on who is searching or what is being sold — not on competition.

Two are held, not blocked. specialty leasing and commercial property management are both real work that Husband Retail does. Neither is turned down on merit — specialty leasing returns landlords letting kiosks to retailers, which is the opposite side of the table from the client, and commercial property management returns a city-by-city fight that a firm with no US offices cannot enter. If US offices open, both come back for a second look.

5. Build order

Easiest first, because two of these score 1 for competition and there is no reason to start anywhere else.

Stage Pages Why here
1/landlord-representation, /tenant-representationcompetition 1 and competition 4. Six of the twenty terms sit on these two pages. /tenant-representation waits on D7-US
2/retail-brokerage, /retail-investment-salescompetition 6 both, and the results pages are pure peer set — CBRE, JLL, Newmark, Marcus & Millichap
3/feasibility-studies, /insights/cam-reconciliationcompetition 8 and 18. CAM reconciliation carries eight paid adverts, the strongest buying signal on the whole list
4/retail-site-selection, /commercial-real-estate-consulting, /retail-property-managementcompetition 15, 15 and 20
5/, /servicesThe home page needs the rest of the tree behind it first
6/retail-leasing, /retail-market-report, /insights/commercial-lease-negotiationcompetition 31, 35 and 19. The market report is a recurring publication, not a one-off build
7The fourteen pages that are not search targetsWritten last, but /contact and /privacy-policy must exist before anything goes live

Paul's answer, 12 August 2026 — D7-US is settled: yes

/tenant-representation is cleared to build, with its three second phrases. At competition 4 it is still the easiest page on the American tree.

The four rows sum to 2,270 American searches a month. The decisions table below says 2,780; the rows support the lower figure, so 2,270 is the one to use until the difference is re-checked.

D8-US and D9-US are not affected. They ask different questions from the British and Australian D8 and D9 — whether the firm is entering the American market at all, and which address goes on the contact page. Both stay open.

D7-US~~Does Husband Retail act for occupiers in the US?~~ Answered yes, 12 Aug 2026 — cleared to build. The Hong Kong dossier says the firm is "not a tenant-rep broker". If that holds in the US, sixteen terms remain and the tree loses one fee-earning pagePaul/tenant-representation and its three secondaries

7. What this tab does not contain

Fifty-six results pages were read for the United States. One hundred and four US terms were measured and not read — they are in the working file, marked "measured, not read", and none of them has a page, a heading or a line of draft copy against it.

Eleven of the 160 terms measured returned no demand at all in any country: shopping center consulting, retail real estate firm, retail property advisory, shopping center consultant, shopping center broker, net lease advisory, retail lease audit, retail portfolio strategy, store closure strategy, retail real estate consultant, mall management company.

That last one is worth reading twice. retail real estate consultant — the job title — has zero searches a month in the United States. So does shopping center consultant. The same thing was true in Hong Kong, and it is the single most reliable finding across both markets: the words an industry uses about itself are not the words its customers type.

Digital Movement · Husband Retail · United States · 160 terms measured, 56 live results pages read, 20 commercially relevant terms confirmed. 12 August 2026.

How to read the States

  • Not built — cleared, nothing blocking it, no page exists yet.
  • Held — decision D7-AU / D8-AU / D9-AU — ~~the search demand is proven and the results page is commercial, but it is unproven whether Husband Retail sells the thing being bought.~~ All three were answered yes on 12 August 2026, so every page in this state is now cleared to build. One condition carries over from D9-AU: the valuation page is written as advisory unless the firm can name a Certified Practising Valuer. See §5.

2. What Australia turned out to be

145 terms measured. 41 live results pages read. 23 terms carry buying intent. 39 terms have no Australian search demand at all.

Australia is a real market with a real problem: there is strong demand for retail property services, and almost no demand for retail property consultancy, which is what Husband Retail sells.

The four findings that shape the tree

1. Generic property words in Australia belong to residential buyer's agents. This is the single biggest trap in the market, and it caught six terms: property advisory (210/mo, A$13.98 a click) returns Wakelin, REBAA and National Property Advisory — buyer's advocates for people purchasing an investment flat. property strategy (210) returns Westpac and NAB. commercial property investment (1,000, competition 13), commercial property strategy (70, competition 4 — the easiest term found anywhere in the market) and retail property investment (70) all return the same beginner's guides: Charter Hall, Rethink Investing, Westbridge Funds, "How to Invest in Commercial Property in Australia". A page built on any of these would rank in front of a private individual buying their first shop.

2. Adding "commercial real estate" fixes it; adding "property" does not. commercial real estate consulting and commercial real estate advisory return CBRE, Savills, Urbis, KPMG, EY, BDO and FTI — institutional advisers with institutional clients. Same shape as the American result. These two phrases are the safest entry into the market despite modest volume.

3. Singular and plural are different search terms. retail leasing agent returns three job listings out of ten. retail leasing agents returns Savills, CBRE, Colliers, Fitzroys, Ainsworth and Cygnet West, and no jobs at all. The same split shows up in retail property consultant (no Australian demand) versus retail property consultants (50/mo, every result a consultancy). Build the plural. Always check both.

4. "Retail agency" means recruitment here too. Nine of ten results are staffing firms — Six Degrees, Frontline Retail, SMAART, Hays, People Equity. The United Kingdom showed the identical pattern this morning. It is not a British quirk; in both markets the phrase belongs to shop-staff hiring. Do not use it in any headline, page title or ad.

The firm's own vocabulary has no Australian demand

Thirty-nine measured phrases returned zero. Among them, every phrase Husband Retail uses to describe itself: retail masterplanning · shopping centre repositioning · retail repositioning · centre repositioning · retail concept development · tenant mix strategy · retail mix · shopping centre consultant · retail development consultant · retail investment advisory · shopping centre asset management · retail property advisory · retail asset strategy · retail landlord · retail planning · retail development · shopping centre development.

This is the third market to produce the same result. Hong Kong found retailer representation at zero; the United States found retail consulting meaning McKinsey. The words on husband-retail.com cannot be used as search targets in any market measured so far. They remain correct as positioning; they are not demand.
